Hi, Doctor. Um… I guess what finally pushed me to come in is work stuff. I’m an accountant, and after audits or even mentorship events, I’ll start these follow-up items with every intention of finishing them—like the final email, a review note, or a form—and then somehow days later I find out it never actually got done.

Yeah, I mean… the clearest place I see it is work. That’s really what pushed me to make the appointment. Like after an audit or a mentorship event, I’ll start the follow-up stuff thinking, okay, I just need to send the final email or write the review note or finish the form, and then somehow a few days go by and I realize it never actually got done.

I’ll even sort of remember being right on the edge of doing it, if that makes sense. But then it’s just… still sitting there unfinished, and someone is waiting on me. That’s the part that’s been getting harder to brush off.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to finish these tasks, do you find yourself easily distracted by other things, or does it feel more like you just lose track of the steps needed to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more the distracted thing, I think. Like if I’m reviewing an audit file and a Slack message pops up, or someone is talking outside my office, I can get pulled completely away from what I was doing.\n\nAnd it’s not always even external—sometimes I’ll have a random thought, like something about marathon training or whether I need new shoes, and then suddenly I’m looking that up or thinking through my running schedule, and the audit file is just… still there. Yes, definitely. Sitting still through a long review can feel… weirdly physical, like there’s pressure building up inside me. I’ll be trying to focus, and then I just feel like I need to get up.

Sometimes I’ll stand up to walk to the printer, even if I don’t really need to, or I’ll invent some reason to move around for a minute. It’s funny, because as an accountant you’d think I’d be good at just sitting with documents, and I can do it, but it feels uncomfortable after a while—like my body is saying, “Okay, enough, move.”

Then later I’m like, wait, I never finished that section.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, as if you need to be moving or doing something, even in situations where others might feel comfortable just sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Yeah, I think so. It’s maybe most obvious with longer things. Like I can start a long client meeting totally engaged, taking notes, following along, and then at some point I realize I’ve been staring at the screen but I didn’t actually hear the last few minutes.

That happened in one of those AI-tool trainings too—everyone’s talking about how it’s going to change audit workflows, and I was interested, honestly, because I do think some of that stuff is useful, though also a little overhyped maybe… but anyway, I suddenly realized I’d missed a whole chunk of what the trainer was saying. Yes. I get really frustrated with myself, honestly. That’s kind of what pushed me to make this appointment.

It’s not that I don’t care—I’ll leave an audit or a mentorship event with every intention of doing the follow-up items. I’ll even start them. And then days later I’ll realize the final email never got sent, or a review note is still sitting unfinished, or some form I meant to submit just… didn’t actually get done.

And then I’m mad at myself because it feels so avoidable. Like, I meant to do it. I can picture myself thinking, “I’ll finish that in a minute,” and then somehow it just disappears until it’s a problem.

Like after an audit or a mentorship event, I’ll start the follow-up stuff thinking, okay, I just need to send the final email or write the review note or finish the form, and then somehow a few days go by and I realize it never actually got done.\n\nIt’s not usually that I don’t care about it—I really do mean to finish it. I’ll even sort of remember being right on the edge of doing it, if that makes sense. But then it’s just… still sitting there unfinished, and someone is waiting on me. That’s the part that’s been getting harder to brush off.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to finish these tasks, do you find yourself easily distracted by other things, or does it feel more like you just lose track of the steps needed to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more the distracted thing, I think. Like if I’m reviewing an audit file and a Slack message pops up, or someone is talking outside my office, I can get pulled completely away from what I was doing.\n\nAnd it’s not always even external—sometimes I’ll have a random thought, like something about marathon training or whether I need new shoes, and then suddenly I’m looking that up or thinking through my running schedule, and the audit file is just… still there. Then later I’m like, wait, I never finished that section.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, as if you need to be moving or doing something, even in situations where others might feel comfortable just sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sitting still through a long review can feel… weirdly physical, like there’s pressure building up inside me. I’ll be trying to focus, and then I just feel like I need to get up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand up to walk to the printer, even if I don’t really need to, or I’ll invent some reason to move around for a minute. It’s funny, because as an accountant you’d think I’d be good at just sitting with documents, and I can do it, but it feels uncomfortable after a while—like my body is saying, “Okay, enough, move.”\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar difficulties with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were younger, like in school or with chores at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early. Even in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through and missed little details. Or I’d talk out of turn and need reminders to actually finish the work.\n\nMy mom still brings it up sometimes—she jokes that I could get an A on the test but forget the whole project sitting in my backpack. Which is funny now, kind of, but also… yeah, that pattern sounds pretty familiar.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to finish tasks or losing focus even in everyday routines, like chores at home, running errands, or even while watching a show or reading something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s maybe most obvious with longer things. Like I can start a long client meeting totally engaged, taking notes, following along, and then at some point I realize I’ve been staring at the screen but I didn’t actually hear the last few minutes.\n\nThat happened in one of those AI-tool trainings too—everyone’s talking about how it’s going to change audit workflows, and I was interested, honestly, because I do think some of that stuff is useful, though also a little overhyped maybe… but anyway, I suddenly realized I’d missed a whole chunk of what the trainer was saying. I was still sitting there looking attentive, but my mind had just slipped off somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel frustrated or upset with yourself when you realize you’ve missed something or didn’t finish a task, even if you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get really frustrated with myself, honestly. That’s kind of what pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care—I’ll leave an audit or a mentorship event with every intention of doing the follow-up items. I’ll even start them. And then days later I’ll realize the final email never got sent, or a review note is still sitting unfinished, or some form I meant to submit just… didn’t actually get done.\n\nAnd then I’m mad at myself because it feels so avoidable. Like, I meant to do it. I can picture myself thinking, “I’ll finish that in a minute,” and then somehow it just disappears until it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to start tasks, even when you know they’re important or when you have a clear plan for how to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “can’t start” from “I get pulled into something else,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike, if I have a plan, sometimes I’ll sit down with all the right stuff open and then somehow I’m checking one related email, and then that leads to another thing, and—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I do notice there are times where I know something matters, but getting myself to actually begin feels weirdly harder than it should.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say it’s every task. Sometimes if there’s pressure or it’s urgent, I can start. It’s more like… the starting line is sticky, maybe. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s been happening enough that I thought, okay, I probably need to talk to someone instead of just buying another planner or making another color-coded list.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of difficulty with follow-through happen only at work, or do you notice similar patterns in other areas of your life, like personal projects, household tasks, or social commitments?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the clearest place I see it is work. That’s really what pushed me to make the appointment. Like after an audit or a mentorship event, I’ll start the follow-up stuff thinking, okay, I just need to send the final email or write the review note or finish the form, and then somehow a few days go by and I realize it never actually got done.\n\nIt’s not usually that I don’t care about it—I really do mean to finish it. I’ll even sort of remember being right on the edge of doing it, if that makes sense. But then it’s just… still sitting there unfinished, and someone is waiting on me. That’s the part that’s been getting harder to brush off.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to finish these tasks, do you find yourself easily distracted by other things, or does it feel more like you just lose track of the steps needed to finish them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s more the distracted thing, I think. Like if I’m reviewing an audit file and a Slack message pops up, or someone is talking outside my office, I can get pulled completely away from what I was doing.\n\nAnd it’s not always even external—sometimes I’ll have a random thought, like something about marathon training or whether I need new shoes, and then suddenly I’m looking that up or thinking through my running schedule, and the audit file is just… still there. Then later I’m like, wait, I never finished that section.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, as if you need to be moving or doing something, even in situations where others might feel comfortable just sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sitting still through a long review can feel… weirdly physical, like there’s pressure building up inside me. I’ll be trying to focus, and then I just feel like I need to get up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand up to walk to the printer, even if I don’t really need to, or I’ll invent some reason to move around for a minute. It’s funny, because as an accountant you’d think I’d be good at just sitting with documents, and I can do it, but it feels uncomfortable after a while—like my body is saying, “Okay, enough, move.”\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar difficulties with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were younger, like in school or with chores at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early. Even in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through and missed little details. Or I’d talk out of turn and need reminders to actually finish the work.\n\nMy mom still brings it up sometimes—she jokes that I could get an A on the test but forget the whole project sitting in my backpack. Which is funny now, kind of, but also… yeah, that pattern sounds pretty familiar.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to finish tasks or losing focus even in everyday routines, like chores at home, running errands, or even while watching a show or reading something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s maybe most obvious with longer things. Like I can start a long client meeting totally engaged, taking notes, following along, and then at some point I realize I’ve been staring at the screen but I didn’t actually hear the last few minutes.\n\nThat happened in one of those AI-tool trainings too—everyone’s talking about how it’s going to change audit workflows, and I was interested, honestly, because I do think some of that stuff is useful, though also a little overhyped maybe… but anyway, I suddenly realized I’d missed a whole chunk of what the trainer was saying. I was still sitting there looking attentive, but my mind had just slipped off somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel frustrated or upset with yourself when you realize you’ve missed something or didn’t finish a task, even if you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get really frustrated with myself, honestly. That’s kind of what pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care—I’ll leave an audit or a mentorship event with every intention of doing the follow-up items. I’ll even start them. And then days later I’ll realize the final email never got sent, or a review note is still sitting unfinished, or some form I meant to submit just… didn’t actually get done.\n\nAnd then I’m mad at myself because it feels so avoidable. Like, I meant to do it. I can picture myself thinking, “I’ll finish that in a minute,” and then somehow it just disappears until it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to start tasks, even when you know they’re important or when you have a clear plan for how to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “can’t start” from “I get pulled into something else,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike, if I have a plan, sometimes I’ll sit down with all the right stuff open and then somehow I’m checking one related email, and then that leads to another thing, and—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I do notice there are times where I know something matters, but getting myself to actually begin feels weirdly harder than it should.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say it’s every task. Sometimes if there’s pressure or it’s urgent, I can start. It’s more like… the starting line is sticky, maybe. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have trouble managing frustration, especially when things don’t go as planned or when you’re trying to focus on something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something gets derailed, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s a major thing I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m more frustrated with the situation than angry, if that makes sense. Like if I’m trying to get something done and there are interruptions, I can feel myself getting tense—but then again, in accounting there are always interruptions, especially around deadlines, and everyone’s tense then. Sorry, that’s probably not a very clear answer.\n\nI guess I haven’t really thought of irritability as the main problem. It’s more that I notice I’m struggling and then I get frustrated after the fact.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I notice it most at work, but it’s not only work.

Like if I’m trying to review an audit file, even a Slack ping or someone talking outside my office can completely pull me off track. Or I’ll be reading something and then suddenly I’m thinking about marathon training—like what pace I’m supposed to run this weekend—and then I realize I have no idea what I just read.

In conversations it can happen too, though I try to hide it. I’ll be listening and then some random thought catches, and I have to kind of mentally rewind. It’s embarrassing, honestly.

Like if I’m trying to get something done and there are interruptions, I can feel myself getting tense—but then again, in accounting there are always interruptions, especially around deadlines, and everyone’s tense then. Sorry, that’s probably not a very clear answer.\n\nI guess I haven’t really thought of irritability as the main problem. It’s more that I notice I’m struggling and then I get frustrated after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and structured tasks, do you find yourself losing track of things or getting distracted in more casual situations—like while having a conversation, watching a movie, or even just trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I notice it most at work, but it’s not only work.\n\nLike if I’m trying to review an audit file, even a Slack ping or someone talking outside my office can completely pull me off track. Or I’ll be reading something and then suddenly I’m thinking about marathon training—like what pace I’m supposed to run this weekend—and then I realize I have no idea what I just read.\n\nIn conversations it can happen too, though I try to hide it. I’ll be listening and then some random thought catches, and I have to kind of mentally rewind. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences, like interrupting others, making sudden decisions, or saying things you later regret?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / accountant_or_auditor / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"I catch myself jumping into conversations before people finish, especially with junior auditors, because the answer or idea feels urgent in my head and I blurt it out before I can stop myself.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that happens, mostly with interrupting. I don’t usually think of myself as making big sudden decisions or anything, but in conversations—especially with junior auditors—I’ll jump in before they’ve finished.

It’s like I can see where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, and it feels urgent in that moment, so I blurt it out. Then afterward I realize I kind of cut them off. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can hear myself doing it sometimes and still don’t stop fast enough.

Or I’ll be reading something and then suddenly I’m thinking about marathon training—like what pace I’m supposed to run this weekend—and then I realize I have no idea what I just read.\n\nIn conversations it can happen too, though I try to hide it. I’ll be listening and then some random thought catches, and I have to kind of mentally rewind. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences, like interrupting others, making sudden decisions, or saying things you later regret?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that happens, mostly with interrupting. I don’t usually think of myself as making big sudden decisions or anything, but in conversations—especially with junior auditors—I’ll jump in before they’ve finished.\n\nIt’s like I can see where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, and it feels urgent in that moment, so I blurt it out. Then afterward I realize I kind of cut them off. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can hear myself doing it sometimes and still don’t stop fast enough.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you notice these same issues with distraction or losing focus in social settings, such as when you're at dinner with friends or attending a gathering?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes, it can happen there too, though I guess I notice it more clearly at work because there’s something I’m supposed to be doing.

Like, the same kind of thing happens if I’m trying to follow a conversation and there’s noise nearby, or even just some random thought pops in. Yeah, I mean… yes, it can happen even when it’s something I’m interested in. Not always in the same way as chores, but I’ll start off totally engaged.

Sitting still through a long review can feel… weirdly physical, like there’s pressure building up inside me. I’ll be trying to focus, and then I just feel like I need to get up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand up to walk to the printer, even if I don’t really need to, or I’ll invent some reason to move around for a minute. It’s funny, because as an accountant you’d think I’d be good at just sitting with documents, and I can do it, but it feels uncomfortable after a while—like my body is saying, “Okay, enough, move.”\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar difficulties with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were younger, like in school or with chores at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think it was there pretty early. Even in elementary school, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I lost assignments or rushed through and missed little details. Or I’d talk out of turn and need reminders to actually finish the work.\n\nMy mom still brings it up sometimes—she jokes that I could get an A on the test but forget the whole project sitting in my backpack. Which is funny now, kind of, but also… yeah, that pattern sounds pretty familiar.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to finish tasks or losing focus even in everyday routines, like chores at home, running errands, or even while watching a show or reading something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s maybe most obvious with longer things. Like I can start a long client meeting totally engaged, taking notes, following along, and then at some point I realize I’ve been staring at the screen but I didn’t actually hear the last few minutes.\n\nThat happened in one of those AI-tool trainings too—everyone’s talking about how it’s going to change audit workflows, and I was interested, honestly, because I do think some of that stuff is useful, though also a little overhyped maybe… but anyway, I suddenly realized I’d missed a whole chunk of what the trainer was saying. I was still sitting there looking attentive, but my mind had just slipped off somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel frustrated or upset with yourself when you realize you’ve missed something or didn’t finish a task, even if you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get really frustrated with myself, honestly. That’s kind of what pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care—I’ll leave an audit or a mentorship event with every intention of doing the follow-up items. I’ll even start them. And then days later I’ll realize the final email never got sent, or a review note is still sitting unfinished, or some form I meant to submit just… didn’t actually get done.\n\nAnd then I’m mad at myself because it feels so avoidable. Like, I meant to do it. I can picture myself thinking, “I’ll finish that in a minute,” and then somehow it just disappears until it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to start tasks, even when you know they’re important or when you have a clear plan for how to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “can’t start” from “I get pulled into something else,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike, if I have a plan, sometimes I’ll sit down with all the right stuff open and then somehow I’m checking one related email, and then that leads to another thing, and—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I do notice there are times where I know something matters, but getting myself to actually begin feels weirdly harder than it should.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say it’s every task. Sometimes if there’s pressure or it’s urgent, I can start. It’s more like… the starting line is sticky, maybe. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have trouble managing frustration, especially when things don’t go as planned or when you’re trying to focus on something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something gets derailed, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s a major thing I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m more frustrated with the situation than angry, if that makes sense. Like if I’m trying to get something done and there are interruptions, I can feel myself getting tense—but then again, in accounting there are always interruptions, especially around deadlines, and everyone’s tense then. Sorry, that’s probably not a very clear answer.\n\nI guess I haven’t really thought of irritability as the main problem. Which is funny now, kind of, but also… yeah, that pattern sounds pretty familiar.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to finish tasks or losing focus even in everyday routines, like chores at home, running errands, or even while watching a show or reading something?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. It’s maybe most obvious with longer things. Like I can start a long client meeting totally engaged, taking notes, following along, and then at some point I realize I’ve been staring at the screen but I didn’t actually hear the last few minutes.\n\nThat happened in one of those AI-tool trainings too—everyone’s talking about how it’s going to change audit workflows, and I was interested, honestly, because I do think some of that stuff is useful, though also a little overhyped maybe… but anyway, I suddenly realized I’d missed a whole chunk of what the trainer was saying. I was still sitting there looking attentive, but my mind had just slipped off somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel frustrated or upset with yourself when you realize you’ve missed something or didn’t finish a task, even if you meant to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get really frustrated with myself, honestly. That’s kind of what pushed me to make this appointment.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care—I’ll leave an audit or a mentorship event with every intention of doing the follow-up items. I’ll even start them. And then days later I’ll realize the final email never got sent, or a review note is still sitting unfinished, or some form I meant to submit just… didn’t actually get done.\n\nAnd then I’m mad at myself because it feels so avoidable. Like, I meant to do it. I can picture myself thinking, “I’ll finish that in a minute,” and then somehow it just disappears until it’s a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to start tasks, even when you know they’re important or when you have a clear plan for how to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate “can’t start” from “I get pulled into something else,” if that makes sense.\n\nLike, if I have a plan, sometimes I’ll sit down with all the right stuff open and then somehow I’m checking one related email, and then that leads to another thing, and—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. I guess I do notice there are times where I know something matters, but getting myself to actually begin feels weirdly harder than it should.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say it’s every task. Sometimes if there’s pressure or it’s urgent, I can start. It’s more like… the starting line is sticky, maybe. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel irritable or have trouble managing frustration, especially when things don’t go as planned or when you’re trying to focus on something important?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something gets derailed, but I don’t know that I’d say it’s a major thing I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes I’m more frustrated with the situation than angry, if that makes sense. Like if I’m trying to get something done and there are interruptions, I can feel myself getting tense—but then again, in accounting there are always interruptions, especially around deadlines, and everyone’s tense then. Sorry, that’s probably not a very clear answer.\n\nI guess I haven’t really thought of irritability as the main problem. It’s more that I notice I’m struggling and then I get frustrated after the fact.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and structured tasks, do you find yourself losing track of things or getting distracted in more casual situations—like while having a conversation, watching a movie, or even just trying to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I notice it most at work, but it’s not only work.\n\nLike if I’m trying to review an audit file, even a Slack ping or someone talking outside my office can completely pull me off track. Or I’ll be reading something and then suddenly I’m thinking about marathon training—like what pace I’m supposed to run this weekend—and then I realize I have no idea what I just read.\n\nIn conversations it can happen too, though I try to hide it. I’ll be listening and then some random thought catches, and I have to kind of mentally rewind. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences, like interrupting others, making sudden decisions, or saying things you later regret?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that happens, mostly with interrupting. I don’t usually think of myself as making big sudden decisions or anything, but in conversations—especially with junior auditors—I’ll jump in before they’ve finished.\n\nIt’s like I can see where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, and it feels urgent in that moment, so I blurt it out. Then afterward I realize I kind of cut them off. I don’t mean to be rude, but I can hear myself doing it sometimes and still don’t stop fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these same issues with distraction or losing focus in social settings, such as when you're at dinner with friends or attending a gathering?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can happen there too, though I guess I notice it more clearly at work because there’s something I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nLike, the same kind of thing happens if I’m trying to follow a conversation and there’s noise nearby, or even just some random thought pops in. Or I’ll suddenly be thinking about marathon training, like what pace I’m supposed to run, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nIn social situations it’s a little harder to describe because everyone’s talking and there’s food and side conversations, so I sort of tell myself that’s normal.
